This lesson shows the relaxed forms friends use with each other.

- `pele-o`: **Pẹ̀lẹ́ o** (Sorry, take it easy)
  - Parts: Pẹ̀lẹ́ + o
  - Doubt: Confirm the tones of pẹ̀lẹ́.
- `se-o-gbo`: **Ṣé o gbọ́** (You hear? Do you agree?)
  - Parts: Ṣé + o + gbọ́
  - Doubt: Confirm the casual use of Ṣé o gbọ́.
- `o-ya`: **Ó yá** (Okay, let's go)
  - Parts: Ó + yá
  - Doubt: Confirm the use of Ó yá for okay.
- `ko-si-wahala`: **Kò sí wàhálà** (No problem)
  - Parts: Kò sí + wàhálà
  - Doubt: Confirm the spelling and tones of wàhálà.
- `se-bi-iwo-ni`: **Ṣebí ìwọ ni** (It is you, right?)
  - Parts: Ṣebí + ìwọ + ni
  - Doubt: Confirm the tones of ṣebí.

## Vocab

### Getting Started

#### Greetings and courtesies (`gs-greetings`)
Sources consulted: src/content/lessons/gs-3.ts, this app's greetings lesson (itself sourced from COERLL Yorùbá Yé Mi and Wiktionary): ẹ káàárọ̀, ẹ káalẹ́, ẹ kúuṣẹ́ and the respectful ẹ. Not rechecked against COERLL for this topic.; Wiktionary, "kaasan" (Yoruba section): káàsán, good afternoon, used between noon and 4 pm; ẹ comes first when speaking respectfully.; Wiktionary, "ẹ ṣeun" (Yoruba section): thank you, honorific or to several people; o ṣeun is the plain form.; Wiktionary, "bawo" (Yoruba section): báwo, how; derived term báwo ni?.; University of Georgia, Yoruba unit 8 culture page "Greetings in Yoruba": E kaabo (welcome, to a senior or a group), written without tone marks.; The model's own knowledge for the tone marks of ẹ káàbọ̀, ẹ jọ̀ọ́, ẹ má bínú and ó dàbọ̀, and for all ten example sentences. No tone-marked dictionary entry for those four was found while drafting.

- `gs-greetings-kaaro`: **ẹ káàárọ̀** (good morning, phrase)
  - Sentence: Ẹ káàárọ̀, oòrùn ti yọ. / Good morning, the sun is up.
  - Doubt: Same form as lesson gs-3. ẹ shows respect; to a friend, káàárọ̀ alone. Sentence drafted by the model: confirm that oòrùn ti yọ (the sun is up) sounds natural.
- `gs-greetings-kaasan`: **ẹ káàsán** (good afternoon, phrase)
  - Sentence: Ẹ káàsán, ọ̀sán yìí gbóná. / Good afternoon, it is hot this afternoon.
  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives káàsán, used from noon to about 4 pm. Sentence drafted by the model: confirm gbóná (hot) for the weather, rather than a phrase with oòrùn (sun).
- `gs-greetings-kaale`: **ẹ káalẹ́** (good evening, phrase)
  - Sentence: Ẹ káalẹ́, alẹ́ yìí tutù. / Good evening, it is cool this evening.
  - Doubt: Same form as lesson gs-3. Confirm the mid tone on the second a. Sentence drafted by the model: confirm tutù (cool, cold) for the evening air.
- `gs-greetings-kuuse`: **ẹ kúuṣẹ́** (well done (to someone working), phrase)
  - Sentence: Ẹ kúuṣẹ́, ẹ ṣì ń kọ́lé. / Well done, you are still building.
  - Doubt: Same joined form as lesson gs-3; some write it as ẹ kú iṣẹ́. Sentence drafted by the model: confirm ṣì ń kọ́lé (still building a house) is natural.
- `gs-greetings-kaabo`: **ẹ káàbọ̀** (welcome, phrase)
  - Sentence: Ẹ káàbọ̀ sí ilé wa. / Welcome to our home.
  - Doubt: Tone marks from the model's own knowledge (kú + àbọ̀, joined like káàárọ̀). The one source checked writes E kaabo without tone marks. Sentence drafted by the model.
- `gs-greetings-seun`: **ẹ ṣeun** (thank you, phrase)
  - Sentence: Ẹ ṣeun fún ẹ̀bùn yìí. / Thank you for this gift.
  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives ẹ ṣeun (o ṣeun to a friend). The shorter ẹ ṣé is also common. Sentence drafted by the model: confirm fún (for) is the natural way to give the reason.
- `gs-greetings-jowo`: **ẹ jọ̀ọ́** (please, phrase)
  - Sentence: Ẹ jọ̀ọ́, ẹ ràn mí lọ́wọ́. / Please help me.
  - Doubt: Tone marks from the model's own knowledge; no source checked. Some write the fuller ẹ jọ̀wọ́. Sentence drafted by the model.
- `gs-greetings-binu`: **ẹ má bínú** (sorry, phrase)
  - Sentence: Ẹ má bínú, mo pẹ́. / Sorry, I am late.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge; no source checked. Literally "do not be angry". Sentence drafted by the model: confirm mo pẹ́ (I am late) is the natural phrasing.
- `gs-greetings-dabo`: **ó dàbọ̀** (goodbye, phrase)
  - Sentence: Ó dàbọ̀, a ó tún rí ara wa. / Goodbye, we will see each other again.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge; no source checked. Confirm the tones and whether it is written as two words or as one (ódàbọ̀). Sentence drafted by the model: confirm a ó (we will) versus a óò.
- `gs-greetings-bawo`: **báwo ni** (how are you?, phrase)
  - Sentence: Báwo ni ara rẹ? / How are you?
  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives báwo (how) and báwo ni?. Casual, for friends; to an elder a greeting with ẹ is more polite. Sentence drafted by the model; báwo ni ara rẹ is literally "how is your body".

#### Family (`gs-family`)
Sources consulted: Wiktionary, "aburo" (Yoruba section): àbúrò, younger sibling, with regional forms àbúò and àbú; coordinate term ẹ̀gbọ́n.; Wiktionary, "ẹgbọn" (Yoruba section): ẹ̀gbọ́n, older sibling, extended to an older relative of the same generation.; src/content/audio-manifest.json and this app's grammar lessons: ọmọ and ọkọ are there as words of their own; bàbá and ọ̀rẹ́ appear only inside phrases (ilé bàbá mi, òun ni ọ̀rẹ́ mi). Same spellings. Not rechecked against an outside source.; The model's own knowledge for ìyá, ìyá àgbà, bàbá àgbà, ìyàwó and for all ten example sentences.

- `gs-family-iya`: **ìyá** (mother, noun)
  - Sentence: Ìyá mi fún mi lọ́mú. / My mother breastfed me.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge. Màmá is also common in everyday speech. Sentence drafted by the model: confirm fún ... lọ́mú (breastfeed) is natural.
- `gs-family-baba`: **bàbá** (father, noun)
  - Sentence: Bàbá mi ni ọkọ ìyá mi. / My father is my mother's husband.
  - Doubt: Same spelling as the grammar lessons (ilé bàbá mi). Some sources write baba with mid tones for the older, more formal form. Sentence drafted by the model.
- `gs-family-omo`: **ọmọ** (child, noun)
  - Sentence: Ìyàwó mi bí ọmọ lánàá. / My wife gave birth to a child yesterday.
  - Doubt: Same spelling as the grammar lessons. Sentence drafted by the model: confirm lánàá (yesterday) and bí (give birth).
- `gs-family-egbon`: **ẹ̀gbọ́n** (older sibling, noun)
  - Sentence: Ẹ̀gbọ́n mi ni àkọ́bí ìyá mi. / My older sibling is my mother's firstborn.
  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives ẹ̀gbọ́n; it covers brother or sister and older cousins too. Sentence drafted by the model: confirm the tones of àkọ́bí (firstborn).
- `gs-family-aburo`: **àbúrò** (younger sibling, noun)
  - Sentence: Àbúrò mi ni àbíkẹ́yìn ìyá mi. / My younger sibling is my mother's last-born.
  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives àbúrò; regional forms differ (àbúò in Oǹdó). Sentence drafted by the model: confirm the tones of àbíkẹ́yìn (last-born).
- `gs-family-iya-agba`: **ìyá àgbà** (grandmother, noun)
  - Sentence: Ìyá àgbà mi ni ìyá bàbá mi. / My grandmother is my father's mother.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge. Ìyá àgbà also means an elderly woman in general; ìyá ìyá is a literal alternative. Sentence drafted by the model.
- `gs-family-baba-agba`: **bàbá àgbà** (grandfather, noun)
  - Sentence: Bàbá àgbà mi ni bàbá ìyá mi. / My grandfather is my mother's father.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge. Bàbá àgbà also means an old man in general. Sentence drafted by the model.
- `gs-family-oko`: **ọkọ** (husband, noun)
  - Sentence: Ọkọ mi ni bàbá ọmọ mi. / My husband is my child's father.
  - Doubt: Same spelling as the grammar lessons (ọkọ mi). Mind the tone pairs ọkọ̀ (vehicle) and ọkọ́ (hoe), not in this topic. Sentence drafted by the model.
- `gs-family-iyawo`: **ìyàwó** (wife, noun)
  - Sentence: Ìyàwó mi ni ìyá ọmọ mi. / My wife is my child's mother.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge. Ìyàwó also means bride, and in-laws use it for a woman married into the family. Sentence drafted by the model.
- `gs-family-ore`: **ọ̀rẹ́** (friend, noun)
  - Sentence: Mo ní ọ̀rẹ́ tuntun ní ilé ìwé. / I have a new friend at school.
  - Doubt: Same spelling as the grammar lessons (òun ni ọ̀rẹ́ mi). Sentence drafted by the model: confirm ilé ìwé versus ilé-ìwé (the grammar lessons use the hyphen in ilé-ìwé lílọ).

#### The body (`gs-body`)
Sources consulted: Wiktionary, "ikun" (Yoruba section): ikùn, abdomen or belly (distinct from ìkùn, a murmur, and ikún, a ground squirrel).; The model's own knowledge for the other nine words and for all ten example sentences. No dictionary entry was checked for them.

- `gs-body-ori`: **orí** (head, noun)
  - Sentence: Orí ń fọ́ mi. / I have a headache.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge. Orí also means destiny. Sentence drafted by the model: orí ń fọ́ mi (literally "head is splitting me") is the usual way to say I have a headache.
- `gs-body-oju`: **ojú** (eye, noun)
  - Sentence: Mo fi ojú mi rí ọ. / I see you with my eyes.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge. Ojú also means face. Sentence drafted by the model.
- `gs-body-eti`: **etí** (ear, noun)
  - Sentence: Mo fi etí mi gbọ́ orin. / I hear music with my ear.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge. Etí also means edge or bank (of a river). Sentence drafted by the model.
- `gs-body-enu`: **ẹnu** (mouth, noun)
  - Sentence: Eyín wà nínú ẹnu mi. / My teeth are in my mouth.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge. Eating was avoided in the sentence because Yoruba people also eat with the hand. Sentence drafted by the model: confirm the tones of eyín (tooth, teeth).
- `gs-body-owo`: **ọwọ́** (hand, noun)
  - Sentence: Mo fi ọwọ́ mi kọ ìwé. / I write a letter with my hand.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge. Ọwọ́ covers hand and arm. Mind owó (money), not in this topic. Sentence drafted by the model.
- `gs-body-ese`: **ẹsẹ̀** (leg, noun)
  - Sentence: Mo fi ẹsẹ̀ mi rìn. / I walk on my legs.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge. Ẹsẹ̀ covers leg and foot. Sentence drafted by the model, same fi ... (use ... to) pattern as the eye, ear, mouth and hand sentences.
- `gs-body-ikun`: **ikùn** (stomach, noun)
  - Sentence: Mo jẹun púpọ̀, ikùn mi wú. / I ate a lot, and my stomach swelled.
  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives ikùn (belly). In everyday speech inú (inside, belly) is often used instead, as in inú mi ń run (I have a stomach ache). Sentence drafted by the model: confirm wú (swell) for a full belly.
- `gs-body-eyin`: **ẹ̀yìn** (back, noun)
  - Sentence: Ìyá pọn ọmọ sí ẹ̀yìn. / The mother ties the baby on her back.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge. Ẹ̀yìn also means behind or after. Mind ẹyin (egg) and ẹ̀yin (you, plural), not in this topic. Sentence drafted by the model: pọn already implies the back, so sí ẹ̀yìn may sound redundant.
- `gs-body-okan`: **ọkàn** (heart, noun)
  - Sentence: Ọkàn mi ń lù. / My heart is beating.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge. Ọkàn also means mind or soul. Sentence drafted by the model: confirm lù (beat) for a heartbeat.
- `gs-body-irun`: **irun** (hair, noun)
  - Sentence: Irun orí mi gùn. / The hair on my head is long.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge. Irun covers body hair too; irun orí is head hair. Sentence drafted by the model.

#### Food and drink (`gs-food`)
Sources consulted: Wiktionary, "ẹwa" (Yoruba section): ẹ̀wà, beans (distinct from ẹwà, beauty, and ẹ̀wá, ten).; Wiktionary, "iyọ" (Yoruba section): iyọ̀, salt.; src/content/audio-manifest.json and this app's grammar lessons: ẹja is there as a word of its own; omi, ẹran and adìyẹ appear only inside phrases (adé ń mu omi, olú mú ọ̀bẹ gé ẹran, adìyẹ tí mo rà). Same spellings. Not rechecked against an outside source.; The model's own knowledge for ìrẹsì, iṣu, búrẹ́dì, ẹyin, èso and for all ten example sentences.

- `gs-food-omi`: **omi** (water, noun)
  - Sentence: Mo mu omi tútù. / I drink cold water.
  - Doubt: Same spelling as the grammar lessons (adé ń mu omi). Sentence drafted by the model: confirm tútù (cold).
- `gs-food-iresi`: **ìrẹsì** (rice, noun)
  - Sentence: Mo se ìrẹsì jọ̀lọ́ọ̀fù. / I cooked jollof rice.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge; confirm the tones of ìrẹsì. Sentence drafted by the model: the spelling jọ̀lọ́ọ̀fù (jollof) is a guess at a loanword with no fixed spelling.
- `gs-food-isu`: **iṣu** (yam, noun)
  - Sentence: Mo fi iṣu gún iyán. / I pounded yam into iyán.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge. Sentence drafted by the model: iyán is pounded yam, made only from yam; confirm fi ... gún (pound with).
- `gs-food-ewa`: **ẹ̀wà** (beans, noun)
  - Sentence: A fi ẹ̀wà ṣe àkàrà. / We make àkàrà from beans.
  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives ẹ̀wà. Mind ẹwà (beauty) and ẹ̀wá (ten), not in this topic. Sentence drafted by the model: àkàrà is a bean fritter.
- `gs-food-buredi`: **búrẹ́dì** (bread, noun)
  - Sentence: Mo fi bọ́tà sí búrẹ́dì. / I put butter on the bread.
  - Doubt: Loanword from English; tones from the model's own knowledge, and spellings vary (búrẹ́dì, búrẹdì). Sentence drafted by the model: bọ́tà (butter) is also a loanword with an unchecked spelling.
- `gs-food-eyin`: **ẹyin** (egg, noun)
  - Sentence: Adìyẹ yé ẹyin. / The hen laid an egg.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge. Mind ẹ̀yin (you, plural) and ẹ̀yìn (back), not in this topic. Sentence drafted by the model: yé is the verb for laying an egg.
- `gs-food-eja`: **ẹja** (fish, noun)
  - Sentence: Mo fi àwọ̀n mú ẹja nínú odò. / I caught fish in the river with a net.
  - Doubt: Same spelling as the grammar lessons (ra ẹja, ẹlẹ́ja). Sentence drafted by the model: confirm the tones of àwọ̀n (net) and mú (catch).

#### Colors (`b-colors`)
Sources consulted: Wiktionary, "pupa" (Yoruba section): red, a noun and a verb (to be red); one of the three basic colors with dúdú and funfun, and it also covers yellow, orange and pink.; Wiktionary, Category:Yoruba colors: lists pupa, funfun, dudu, ofefee and others (most without tone marks).; Wiktionary, "ofefee" (Yoruba section): òféfèé, yellow, with synonyms yẹ́lò and ìyeyè.; Wiktionary, "aro" (Yoruba section): aró, dye, especially indigo dye, and by extension dark blue or indigo (synonym ẹ̀lú).; Wiktionary, "eeru" (Yoruba section): eérú, ash, and grey, with the synonym àwọ̀ eérú.; Wiktionary, "awọ" (Yoruba section): àwọ̀, colour (distinct from awọ, skin).; Wiktionary, "blue" translations: búlúù (a borrowing) and dúdú (general term for dark colors).; Wiktionary, "brown" translations: pupa, which is why brown is not in this topic.; Wiktionary, "purple" translations: written there as àwo elésè àlùkò, with no entry of its own; Wiktionary "aluko" gives àlùkò as a turaco bird.; The model's own knowledge for àwọ̀ ewé, àwọ̀ ọsàn, the tones of dúdú and funfun, and for all ten example sentences.

Sources consulted: Wiktionary, "ilu" (Yoruba section): ìlú, a town or city (also a city-state, a country); ìlù is a drum.; Wiktionary, "abule" (Yoruba section): abúlé, village, hut, cottage.; Wiktionary, "afara" (Yoruba section): afárá, bridge; afàrà is the limba tree.; Wiktionary, "osi" (Yoruba section): òsì, left; ọwọ́ òsì, left hand.; src/content/lessons (the vowel lessons, citing COERLL Yorùbá Yé Mi page 304 and Wiktionary "ọkọ"): ọkọ̀, vehicle. Same spelling. Not rechecked for this topic.; Wiktionary had no Yoruba entry at "ibudoko" and no Yoruba section in the part of "ona" that loaded.   - Doubt: Tones from Wiktionary. Sentence drafted by the model: confirm dá oko (to farm) and kúrò ní (leave); ìlú is named as the place he left, so it cannot fill the blank.
- `i-town-oja`: **ọjà** (market, noun)

### Advanced

#### Market and money (`a-market`)
Sources consulted: Wiktionary, "gbese" (Yoruba section): gbèsè, debt.; Wiktionary, "ere" (Yoruba section): èrè, advantage, reward, profit (eré is play, Ère a statue).; src/content/lessons/i-3.ts sources (citing COERLL Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 9 vocabulary, page 212): oníṣòwò, trader. The model's own knowledge for oníbàárà, iye owó, ṣẹ́ǹjì, àdánù, báǹkì, olówó and tálákà, and for all ten example sentences.

- `a-market-onibaara`: **oníbàárà** (customer, noun)
  - Sentence: Ẹni tí ó ń ra ọjà lọ́wọ́ oníṣòwò ni a ń pè ní oníbàárà. / A person who buys goods from a trader is called a customer.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge, not found on Wiktionary; confirm the tones. Sentence drafted by the model: a definition, so only customer fits; confirm pè ní (call, name).
- `a-market-onisowo`: **oníṣòwò** (trader, noun)
  - Sentence: Oníṣòwò máa ń ra ọjà ní owó kékeré, kí ó lè tà á ní owó tí ó ju bẹ́ẹ̀ lọ. / A trader buys goods cheaply so that he can sell them for more.
  - Doubt: Same spelling as the COERLL list quoted in the grammar lesson sources. Sentence drafted by the model: buying to resell defines a trader, not a customer or a rich person; confirm ní owó kékeré (cheaply) and ju bẹ́ẹ̀ lọ (more than that).
- `a-market-iye-owo`: **iye owó** (price, noun)
  - Sentence: Mo béèrè iye owó bàtà náà lọ́wọ́ oníṣòwò kí n tó rà á. / I asked the trader the price of the shoes before I bought them.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (literally amount of money); speakers often just say owó or ask Èló ni? Sentence drafted by the model: asking before buying rules out change, profit and debt; confirm béèrè ... lọ́wọ́ (ask someone) and rà á.
- `a-market-senji`: **ṣẹ́ǹjì** (change (money given back), noun)
  - Sentence: Mo fún un ní ẹgbẹ̀rún náírà fún ọjà ẹgbẹ̀rin náírà, ó sì dá ṣẹ́ǹjì igba náírà padà fún mi. / I gave him a thousand naira for goods costing eight hundred naira, and he gave me back two hundred naira in change.
  - Doubt: A loanword from English change, from the model's own knowledge; confirm the spelling and tones and whether a native word is preferred. Sentence drafted by the model: the arithmetic singles out change; confirm ẹgbẹ̀rún (1000), ẹgbẹ̀rin (800), igba (200) and dá ... padà (give back).
- `a-market-ere`: **èrè** (profit, noun)
  - Sentence: Ó ra ọjà náà ní ọgọ́rùn-ún náírà, ó tà á ní igba náírà, nítorí náà èrè rẹ̀ jẹ́ ọgọ́rùn-ún náírà. / She bought the goods for one hundred naira and sold them for two hundred, so her profit was one hundred naira.
  - Doubt: Tones from Wiktionary. Sentence drafted by the model: selling above cost rules out àdánù (loss); the verb jèrè (make a profit) was avoided because it contains èrè. Confirm ọgọ́rùn-ún (100).
- `a-market-adanu`: **àdánù** (loss, noun)
  - Sentence: Ó ra ọjà náà ní igba náírà, ṣùgbọ́n ó tà á ní ọgọ́rùn-ún náírà, nítorí náà ó ṣe àdánù. / She bought the goods for two hundred naira but sold them for one hundred, so she made a loss.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge, not found on Wiktionary; confirm the tones. Sentence drafted by the model: selling below cost rules out èrè; confirm ṣe àdánù (make a loss). The usual verb pàdánù (lose) was avoided because it contains àdánù.
- `a-market-gbese`: **gbèsè** (debt, noun)
  - Sentence: Mo yá ẹgbẹ̀rún náírà lọ́wọ́ rẹ̀ ní oṣù tó kọjá, mo sì ti san gbèsè náà padà. / I borrowed a thousand naira from him last month, and I have paid the debt back.
  - Doubt: Tones from Wiktionary. Sentence drafted by the model: borrowing and paying back singles out debt; confirm yá (borrow) and san ... padà (pay back).
- `a-market-banki`: **báǹkì** (bank, noun)
  - Sentence: Mo máa ń kó owó mi pamọ́ sí báǹkì kí àwọn olè má bàa jí i. / I keep my money in the bank so that thieves do not steal it.
  - Doubt: A loanword, spelled as in the Intermediate work topic sentence; confirm the spelling. Sentence drafted by the model: a place to keep money rules out the people words; confirm kó ... pamọ́ (keep safe) and má bàa (so as not to).
- `a-market-olowo`: **olówó** (rich person, noun)
  - Sentence: Bàbá yẹn jẹ́ olówó, owó rẹ̀ pọ̀ débi pé kò sí ohun tí kò lè rà. / That man is a rich person; he has so much money that there is nothing he cannot buy.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (literally owner of money). Sentence drafted by the model: having so much money is the definition, which a trader or customer need not meet; confirm débi pé (so much that).

The model's own knowledge for òtítọ́, ọgbọ́n, ìdájọ́ òdodo, ìmọ̀ and ìgbàgbọ́, and for all ten example sentences.

  - Sentence: Òdìkejì ìwà ahun ni ìlawọ́. / The opposite of stinginess is generosity.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (literally opening of the hand); confirm the form and tones, and whether ọ̀làwọ́ (a generous person) is more usual. Cultural note: generosity to guests and the needy is a mark of good ìwà. Sentence drafted by the model: the opposite of ìwà ahun (stingy behavior) singles out generosity; confirm ahun.
- `m-virtues-ifarada`: **ìfaradà** (endurance, noun)
  - Sentence: Ó fi ìfaradà ṣiṣẹ́ àṣekára fún ọdún mẹ́wàá, kò jáwọ́ títí ó fi kọ́ ilé tirẹ̀. / With endurance she worked hard for ten years and did not give up until she built her own house.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (from fara da, bear with the body); confirm the tones. Cultural note: bearing hardship without complaint is admired, as in the well-known saying Sùúrù ni baba ìwà (patience is the father of character). Sentence drafted by the model: ten years of hard work without giving up singles out endurance, not courage; confirm jáwọ́ (give up).

#### Law and custom (`m-custom`)
Sources consulted: Wiktionary, "ofin" (Yoruba section): òfin, law, rule, legislation.; Wiktionary, "ogun" (Yoruba section): ogún, heritage, inheritance, legacy; also ogún, twenty, and ogun, war.; Wiktionary, "eewo" gave only eéwo (boil, blister), not taboo; èèwọ̀ was not found. Wiktionary returned no page for àṣà.; The model's own knowledge for àṣà, adájọ́, ilé ẹjọ́, ẹ̀wọ̀n, ìbúra, èèwọ̀, ìgbéyàwó and ìsọmọlórúkọ, for the naming-day custom, and for all ten example sentences.

- `m-custom-ofin`: **òfin** (law, noun)
  - Sentence: Ìjọba ti ṣe òfin tuntun pé kí ẹnikẹ́ni má mu sìgá ní gbangba. / The government has made a new law that no one may smoke in public.
  - Doubt: Tones from Wiktionary, which also gives rule. Sentence drafted by the model: a government makes laws, not customs or taboos; confirm mu sìgá (smoke) and ní gbangba (in public).
- `m-custom-asa`: **àṣà** (custom, tradition, noun)
  - Sentence: Kò sí òfin tó pa á láṣẹ, ṣùgbọ́n àṣà Yorùbá ni pé kí ọmọdé kí àgbà ní àárọ̀. / No law commands it, but it is Yoruba custom that a young person greets elders in the morning.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge, not found on Wiktionary; confirm the tones. Sentence drafted by the model: òfin is ruled out in the sentence itself, and a greeting habit is not a taboo (a taboo forbids something); confirm pa ... láṣẹ (command).
- `m-custom-adajo`: **adájọ́** (judge, noun)
  - Sentence: Adájọ́ náà gbọ́ ẹ̀rí àwọn méjèèjì kí ó tó dá ẹjọ́ náà. / The judge heard the evidence of both sides before deciding the case.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (one who decides cases); confirm the tones. Sentence drafted by the model: the only person word in the topic, and hearing evidence then deciding is what a judge does; confirm ẹ̀rí (evidence) and dá ẹjọ́ (decide a case).
- `m-custom-ile-ejo`: **ilé ẹjọ́** (court, noun)
  - Sentence: Wọ́n mú olè náà lọ sí ilé ẹjọ́, adájọ́ sì rán an lọ sí ẹ̀wọ̀n. / They took the thief to court, and the judge sent him to prison.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (literally house of cases); confirm whether ilé-ẹjọ́ is written with a hyphen. Sentence drafted by the model: prison and judge are named in the sentence, so only court fits the place the thief goes first.
- `m-custom-ewon`: **ẹ̀wọ̀n** (prison, noun)
  - Sentence: Wọ́n sọ ọkùnrin náà sí ẹ̀wọ̀n fún ọdún márùn-ún nítorí pé ó jalè. / They put the man in prison for five years because he stole.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (also chain); confirm the tones. Sentence drafted by the model: being held for five years singles out prison, not court; confirm sọ ... sí ẹ̀wọ̀n (throw into prison) and jalè (steal).
- `m-custom-ibura`: **ìbúra** (oath, noun)
  - Sentence: Kí ẹlẹ́rìí náà tó sọ̀rọ̀ ní ilé ẹjọ́, ó ṣe ìbúra pé òtítọ́ nìkan ni òun yóò sọ. / Before the witness spoke in court, he took an oath that he would tell only the truth.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (from búra, swear); confirm the form, since the verb búra alone may be more natural. Sentence drafted by the model: promising to tell only the truth singles out oath; court is named in the sentence.
- `m-custom-eewo`: **èèwọ̀** (taboo, noun)
  - Sentence: Ohun tí àṣà ìbílẹ̀ kò gbà kí ẹnikẹ́ni ṣe ni a ń pè ní èèwọ̀. / Something traditional custom does not allow anyone to do is called a taboo.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge; Wiktionary's eewo page has only eéwo (boil), so confirm the tones. Cultural note: èèwọ̀ often differ by town, family or òrìṣà. Sentence drafted by the model: a definition; a law comes from government, not custom.
- `m-custom-igbeyawo`: **ìgbéyàwó** (marriage, wedding, noun)
  - Sentence: Ní ìgbéyàwó ìbílẹ̀, ẹbí ọkọ máa ń kó ẹ̀bùn lọ sí ilé ẹbí ìyàwó. / At a traditional wedding, the husband's family takes gifts to the bride's family's house.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (literally carrying a wife); confirm the tones. Sentence drafted by the model: husband, bride and gifts single out the wedding, not the naming ceremony; confirm ẹ̀bùn (gift) and ìbílẹ̀ (traditional).
- `m-custom-isomolooruko`: **ìsọmọlórúkọ** (naming ceremony, noun)
  - Sentence: Ní ọjọ́ kẹjọ lẹ́yìn tí a bí ọmọ, ẹbí máa ń péjọ fún ìsọmọlórúkọ. / On the eighth day after a child is born, the family gathers for the naming ceremony.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (literally giving a child a name); confirm the spelling. Cultural note: the eighth day is common today; older sources give the seventh day for girls, the ninth for boys and the eighth for twins. Sentence drafted by the model: a newborn singles out the naming ceremony.
- `m-custom-ogun`: **ogún** (inheritance, noun)
  - Sentence: Nígbà tí bàbá wọn kú, àwọn ọmọ rẹ̀ pín ilé àti ilẹ̀ rẹ̀ gẹ́gẹ́ bí ogún. / When their father died, his children shared out his house and land as their inheritance.
  - Doubt: Tones from Wiktionary. The same spelling also means twenty, and ogun (no mark) is war. Sentence drafted by the model: dividing a dead father's property singles out inheritance; confirm pín (share out) and gẹ́gẹ́ bí (as).

#### Idioms (`m-idioms`)
Sources consulted: Web search for fàáké kọ́rí and fọ̀rọ̀ lọ̀ found no source defining either; fàáké kọ́rí was dropped for that reason. headteacher.ng (lesson note on àkànlò èdè) and discoveryoruba.com idiom lists were read; neither lists the ten idioms below.; The model's own knowledge for all ten idioms, their literal and real meanings, and all ten example sentences. None was confirmed in a dictionary.

- `m-idioms-inu-bi`: **inú bí** (to get angry, phrase)
  - Sentence: Inú bí bàbá mi gidigidi nígbà tí mo fọ́ fèrèsé rẹ̀, ó sì pariwo mọ́ mi. / My father got very angry when I broke his window, and he shouted at me.
  - Doubt: Real meaning: someone becomes angry (the angry person follows: inú bí i, he got angry). Literal: the inside gave birth (or the inside was provoked). From the model's own knowledge, not confirmed in a dictionary; ìbínú (anger) appears to come from it. Sentence drafted by the model: shouting after a broken window singles out anger.
- `m-idioms-oju-ti`: **ojú tì** (to feel ashamed, phrase)
  - Sentence: Ojú tì í gan-an nígbà tí wọ́n mú un pé ó ń purọ́, kò lè wo ẹnikẹ́ni lójú. / He felt very ashamed when they caught him lying; he could not look anyone in the face.
  - Doubt: Real meaning: someone feels ashamed (ojú tì mí, I am ashamed). Literal: the eye (face) closed. From the model's own knowledge, not confirmed in a dictionary; ìtìjú (shame) appears related. Sentence drafted by the model: not being able to look at people singles out shame, not anger; confirm mú un pé (caught him that).
- `m-idioms-fese-fe`: **fẹsẹ̀ fẹ** (to flee, take to one's heels, phrase)
  - Sentence: Nígbà tí olè náà rí ọlọ́pàá, ó fẹsẹ̀ fẹ kí wọ́n má bàa mú un. / When the thief saw the police, he took to his heels so that they would not catch him.
  - Doubt: Real meaning: to run away. Literal: to fan (or widen) with the legs (fi ẹsẹ̀ fẹ). From the model's own knowledge, not confirmed in a dictionary; confirm the tones and the literal reading. Sentence drafted by the model: a thief escaping the police singles out fleeing.
- `m-idioms-foju-di`: **fojú di** (to underestimate, look down on, phrase)
  - Sentence: Má fojú di ọmọdé náà; bí ó tilẹ̀ kéré, ó lè ṣẹ́gun rẹ nínú ayò. / Do not underestimate that child; although he is small, he can beat you at ayò.
  - Doubt: Real meaning: to belittle or underrate someone. Literal: to block (someone) with the eye (fi ojú di); the model is unsure of the literal reading. From the model's own knowledge, not confirmed in a dictionary; the exact form is doubtful (fojú di, fi ojú di, or with an object between), so confirm it. Sentence drafted by the model: small but able to win singles out underestimating; ayò is the Yoruba board game.
- `m-idioms-feti-sile`: **fetí sílẹ̀** (to listen carefully, phrase)
  - Sentence: Ẹ fetí sílẹ̀ dáadáa sí ohun tí olùkọ́ ń sọ, kí ẹ lè dáhùn ìbéèrè rẹ̀. / Listen carefully to what the teacher is saying, so that you can answer his questions.
  - Doubt: Real meaning: to listen, pay attention. Literal: to put the ear down (fi etí sílẹ̀). From the model's own knowledge, not confirmed in a dictionary. Sentence drafted by the model: attending to a speaker in order to answer singles out listening.
- `m-idioms-gbenu-so`: **gbẹnu sọ** (to speak on behalf of, phrase)
  - Sentence: Nítorí pé àwọn ará abúlé kò lè lọ sí ìpàdé náà, olóyè wọn ló gbẹnu sọ fún wọn. / Because the villagers could not go to the meeting, their chief spoke on their behalf.
  - Doubt: Real meaning: to be someone's spokesperson (agbẹnusọ, spokesperson, is from it). Literal: to take up the mouth and speak (gbé ẹnu sọ). From the model's own knowledge, not confirmed in a dictionary; confirm whether it is written gbẹnusọ as one word. Sentence drafted by the model: speaking for people who are absent singles out this idiom.
- `m-idioms-oju-n-pon`: **ojú ń pọ́n** (to be suffering hardship, phrase)
  - Sentence: Láti ìgbà tí ó ti pàdánù iṣẹ́ rẹ̀, ojú ń pọ́n ọn; kò rí owó ra oúnjẹ. / Since he lost his job he has been suffering hardship; he cannot find money to buy food.
  - Doubt: Real meaning: someone is going through hardship (ojú ń pọ́n mi, I am suffering). Literal: the eye is turning red. From the model's own knowledge, not confirmed in a dictionary; ìpọ́njú (hardship) appears related. Sentence drafted by the model: losing a job and having no money for food singles out hardship.
- `m-idioms-fokan-bale`: **fọkàn balẹ̀** (to rest assured, be at ease, phrase)
  - Sentence: Fọkàn balẹ̀, ọmọ rẹ ti dé láìléwu. / Rest assured, your child has arrived safely.
  - Doubt: Real meaning: stop worrying, be calm. Literal: let the heart land (fi ọkàn balẹ̀). From the model's own knowledge, not confirmed in a dictionary. Sentence drafted by the model: news that a child arrived safely singles out reassurance; confirm láìléwu (safely).
- `m-idioms-erin-wo`: **erin wó** (a great person has died, phrase)
  - Sentence: Nígbà tí ọba ìlú wa kú, àwọn ará ìlú ń sọ pé erin wó. / When the king of our town died, the townspeople were saying that a great person had died.
  - Doubt: Real meaning: a king or great person has died. Literal: the elephant has fallen. From the model's own knowledge, not confirmed in a dictionary; it is said to be used out of respect instead of saying kú (die) of a great person. Sentence drafted by the model: the death of a king singles out this idiom.
- `m-idioms-foro-lo`: **fọ̀rọ̀ lọ̀** (to consult, phrase)
  - Sentence: Kí ó tó ra ilé náà, ó fọ̀rọ̀ lọ̀ bàbá rẹ̀ láti gbọ́ èrò rẹ̀. / Before he bought the house, he consulted his father to hear his opinion.
  - Doubt: Real meaning: to ask someone's advice. Literal: to grind a matter with someone (fi ọ̀rọ̀ lọ̀). From the model's own knowledge, not confirmed in a dictionary; a web search found no source. The exact form is doubtful (fọ̀rọ̀ lọ̀ or the unjoined fi ọ̀rọ̀ lọ̀), and so is whether it is common. Confirm, or replace it. Sentence drafted by the model: asking a father's opinion before buying singles out consulting.

#### Words from the proverbs (`m-proverbs`)
Sources consulted: src/content/lessons/m-4.ts: the proverbs Ọmọ tí a kò kọ́ ni yóò gbé ilé tí a kọ́ tà (Afolayan 2021) and Òní l'a rí, ọba òkè l'ó rọ́la (Wiktionary "oni", citing the Global Yoruba Lexical Database); kọ́ (teach, build), gbé, ọba, ọba òkè (the Most High), and pa òwe (COERLL p. 262). Same spellings. Not rechecked for this topic.; src/content/lessons/m-2.ts: the saying Ìmọ́tótó ló lè ṣẹ́gun àrùn gbogbo (COERLL), source of ìmọ́tótó, ṣẹ́gun and àrùn. Same spellings. Not rechecked for this topic.; The model's own knowledge for òwe alone, kọ́lé, and all ten example sentences (new sentences, not the proverbs).

- `m-proverbs-owe`: **òwe** (proverb, noun)
  - Sentence: Òwe ni ọ̀rọ̀ kúkúrú tí àwọn àgbà fi ń kọ́ni ní ọgbọ́n. / A proverb is a short saying that elders use to teach wisdom.
  - Doubt: As in the Mastery 4 lesson title. Sentence drafted by the model: a definition, so no other word fits; confirm kọ́ni (teach people).
- `m-proverbs-pa-owe`: **pa òwe** (cite a proverb, verb)
  - Sentence: Nígbà tí àgbà náà fẹ́ gbà wá nímọ̀ràn, ó pa òwe kan fún wa. / When the elder wanted to advise us, he cited a proverb for us.
  - Doubt: COERLL (quoted in the Mastery 4 lesson sources) gives pa òwe, to say proverbially. Sentence drafted by the model: an elder giving advice singles out citing a proverb; confirm gbà ... nímọ̀ràn (advise).
- `m-proverbs-ko`: **kọ́** (teach, verb)
  - Sentence: Ìyá mi ló kọ́ mi bí a ṣe ń se ọbẹ̀ ẹ̀gúsí. / It was my mother who taught me how to cook egusi soup.
  - Doubt: The teach sense of kọ́ from the first Mastery 4 proverb (the child we did not teach). Sentence drafted by the model: showing someone how to cook singles out teach, not build or carry. Confirm ló (ni ó) and se ọbẹ̀ (cook soup).
- `m-proverbs-kole`: **kọ́lé** (build a house, verb)
  - Sentence: Bàbá mi fẹ́ kọ́lé tirẹ̀, nítorí náà ó ra bíríkì àti sìmẹ́ǹtì. / My father wants to build his own house, so he bought bricks and cement.
  - Doubt: The model's own knowledge: kọ́ (build) + ilé (house) joined, the build sense in the same proverb (the house we built). Confirm the form. Sentence drafted by the model: bricks and cement single out building; confirm the loanwords bíríkì and sìmẹ́ǹtì.
- `m-proverbs-gbe`: **gbé** (carry, verb)
  - Sentence: Ó gbé ẹrù wúwo náà lórí lọ sí ọjà. / She carried the heavy load on her head to the market.
  - Doubt: From the first proverb (gbé ... tà, take and sell) and Mastery 2 (gbé igi, carry the stick). gbé also means live (dwell). Sentence drafted by the model: a load on the head singles out carry; confirm lórí (on the head).
- `m-proverbs-oba`: **ọba** (king, noun)
  - Sentence: Ọba ìlú wa ń gbé ní ààfin, adé sì wà ní orí rẹ̀. / The king of our town lives in the palace, and a crown is on his head.
  - Doubt: As taught in Intermediate 2 and used in the second Mastery 4 proverb. Sentence drafted by the model: a palace and a crown single out king, not the Most High; confirm ààfin (palace) and adé (crown).
- `m-proverbs-oba-oke`: **ọba òkè** (the Most High (God), noun)
  - Sentence: Kò sí ẹni tí ó mọ ohun tí ọ̀la yóò mú wá àfi ọba òkè, ẹni tí ó dá ayé. / No one knows what tomorrow will bring except the Most High, who created the world.
  - Doubt: From the second Mastery 4 proverb, where Wiktionary translates it the Most High (literally king above). Sentence drafted by the model: knowing tomorrow and creating the world single it out, echoing the proverb without copying it; an earthly king does not fit. Confirm àfi (except).
- `m-proverbs-imototo`: **ìmọ́tótó** (cleanliness, noun)
  - Sentence: Fífọ ọwọ́, gbígbá ilé àti wíwẹ̀ déédéé jẹ́ ara ìmọ́tótó. / Washing hands, sweeping the house and bathing regularly are part of cleanliness.
  - Doubt: From the COERLL saying in Mastery 2 (a health saying rather than a traditional proverb). Sentence drafted by the model: washing, sweeping and bathing single out cleanliness; confirm the verbal nouns fífọ, gbígbá and wíwẹ̀.
- `m-proverbs-segun`: **ṣẹ́gun** (defeat, overcome, verb)
  - Sentence: Ẹgbẹ́ wa ṣẹ́gun ẹgbẹ́ wọn nínú ìdíje bọ́ọ̀lù, a sì gba ife ẹ̀yẹ. / Our team defeated their team in the football match, and we won the trophy.
  - Doubt: From the COERLL saying in Mastery 2 (cleanliness can overcome all disease). Sentence drafted by the model: winning a match and a trophy singles out defeat; confirm ìdíje (competition) and ife ẹ̀yẹ (trophy).
- `m-proverbs-arun`: **àrùn** (disease, noun)
  - Sentence: Àrùn yìí máa ń ràn láti ara ẹnì kan sí ẹlòmíràn, nítorí náà wọ́n ya àwọn aláìsàn sọ́tọ̀. / This disease spreads from one person to another, so they kept the sick apart.
  - Doubt: From the COERLL saying in Mastery 2. Sentence drafted by the model: spreading between people singles out disease; confirm ràn (spread, infect) and ya ... sọ́tọ̀ (separate).

- `m-casual-jeje`: **jẹ́jẹ́** (gently, calmly, adverb)
  - Sentence: Ó ń rìn jẹ́jẹ́ nítorí ọmọ ń sùn. / He walks gently because the child is sleeping.

- `m-casual-kuku`: **kúkú** (actually, indeed, after all, adverb)

## Sound Bank

- `v-a`: **a** (vowels): like 'a' in 'father'
  - Examples: àga (chair), ajá (dog), àná (yesterday)
  - Doubt: English comparison approximate.
- `v-e`: **e** (vowels): like 'e' in 'they'
  - Examples: erin (elephant), ejò (snake), etí (ear)
  - Doubt: Check the comparison; Yoruba e is a pure vowel.
- `v-ee`: **ẹ** (vowels): like 'e' in 'bed'
  - Examples: ẹja (fish), ẹran (meat), ẹyin (egg)
  - Doubt: English comparison approximate.
- `v-i`: **i** (vowels): like 'ee' in 'see', shorter
  - Examples: ilé (house), irun (hair), ikùn (stomach)
  - Doubt: English comparison approximate.
- `v-o`: **o** (vowels): like 'o' in 'go', without the glide
  - Examples: omi (water), orí (head), ojú (eye)
  - Doubt: Check the comparison; Yoruba o is a pure vowel.
- `v-oo`: **ọ** (vowels): like 'aw' in 'saw'
  - Examples: ọmọ (child), ọkọ (husband), ọwọ́ (hand)
  - Doubt: English comparison approximate (varies by accent).
- `v-u`: **u** (vowels): like 'oo' in 'moon', shorter
  - Examples: iṣu (yam), ẹnu (mouth), ìlú (town)
  - Doubt: English comparison approximate.
- `n-an`: **an** (vowels): a nasal 'a', like the vowel in French 'blanc'
  - Examples: ọkàn (heart), ẹran (meat)
  - Doubt: Nasal vowel; check example words and the French comparison. In Standard Yoruba an is often said like ọn, so the French comparison is approximate.
- `n-en`: **ẹn** (vowels): a nasal 'ẹ', like the vowel in French 'vin'
  - Examples: ìyẹn (that one), yẹn (that, as in ilé yẹn)
  - Doubt: Rare and debated nasal vowel; example words are my least certain, a native speaker must confirm or replace them.
- `n-in`: **in** (vowels): a nasal 'i', like English 'ee' said through the nose
  - Examples: erin (elephant), mẹ́rin (four)
  - Doubt: Check comparison and example words.
- `n-un`: **un** (vowels): a nasal 'u', like English 'oo' said through the nose
  - Examples: irun (hair), ikùn (stomach)
  - Doubt: Check comparison and example words.
- `n-on`: **ọn** (vowels): a nasal 'ọ', like the vowel in French 'bon'
  - Examples: ẹ̀gbọ́n (older sibling), ìbọn (gun)
  - Doubt: Check comparison and example words.
- `c-b`: **b** (consonants): like 'b' in 'bat'
  - Examples: bàbá (father), bàtà (shoe), ọ̀bọ (monkey)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-d`: **d** (consonants): like 'd' in 'dog'
  - Examples: dúdú (black), adìyẹ (chicken), ọdún (year)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-f`: **f** (consonants): like 'f' in 'fun'
  - Examples: funfun (white), fìlà (cap), fèrèsé (window)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-g`: **g** (consonants): like 'g' in 'go'
  - Examples: gèlè (head wrap), àga (chair)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-gb`: **gb** (consonants): one sound made by saying 'g' and 'b' together, lips closed
  - Examples: agbádá (agbada robe), ẹ̀gbọ́n (older sibling), ológbò (cat)
  - Doubt: Single sound, not g then b. Check the description and examples.
- `c-h`: **h** (consonants): like 'h' in 'hat'
  - Examples: ahọ́n (tongue), ihò (hole)
  - Doubt: Check example words; h is not common.
- `c-j`: **j** (consonants): like 'j' in 'jam'
  - Examples: ejò (snake), ọjọ́ (day)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-k`: **k** (consonants): like 'k' in 'kit'
  - Examples: kọ́kọ́rọ́ (key), ikùn (stomach), ọkọ (husband)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-l`: **l** (consonants): like 'l' in 'lamp'
  - Examples: ilé (house), ọ̀la (tomorrow), alẹ́ (night)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-m`: **m** (consonants): like 'm' in 'man'
  - Examples: omi (water), méjì (two), ọmọ (child)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-n`: **n** (consonants): like 'n' in 'net'
  - Examples: ẹnu (mouth), òní (today), àná (yesterday)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-p`: **p** (consonants): written p but said like 'kp': 'k' and 'p' together, lips closed
  - Examples: pupa (red), àpò (bag)
  - Doubt: Yoruba p is a kp sound; check description and examples.
- `c-r`: **r** (consonants): a quick tap of the tongue, like the 'tt' in American 'butter'
  - Examples: erin (elephant), ọ̀rẹ́ (friend)
  - Doubt: English comparison approximate.
- `c-s`: **s** (consonants): like 's' in 'sun'
  - Examples: sùn (sleep), èso (fruit), ọ̀sẹ̀ (week)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-sh`: **ṣ** (consonants): like 'sh' in 'ship'
  - Examples: aṣọ (cloth), ṣòkòtò (trousers), iṣu (yam)
  - Doubt: Check; do not confuse with s.
- `c-t`: **t** (consonants): like 't' in 'top'
  - Examples: tábìlì (table), etí (ear), mẹ́ta (three)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-w`: **w** (consonants): like 'w' in 'water'
  - Examples: ọwọ́ (hand), ẹ̀wù (garment), wàrà (milk)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-y`: **y** (consonants): like 'y' in 'yes'
  - Examples: ẹyin (egg), yàrá (room), ìyá (mother)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `p-oko-tone`: minimal pair (tone): Same letters, three tones, three meanings.
  - Words: ọkọ (husband), ọkọ̀ (vehicle), ọkọ́ (hoe)
  - Doubt: Check all three meanings and tones.
- `p-igba-tone`: minimal pair (tone): Same letters, three tones, three meanings.
  - Words: igba (two hundred), ìgbà (time), igbá (calabash)
  - Doubt: Native speaker to check the tones of all three words (igba, ìgbà, igbá) and their meanings.
- `p-eyin-tone`: minimal pair (tone): Same letters, different tones and vowel marks.
  - Words: ẹyin (egg), eyín (tooth), ẹ̀yìn (back)
  - Doubt: eyín tone is a known doubt.
- `p-apa-tone`: minimal pair (tone): Only the tone changes.
  - Words: apá (arm), àpá (scar)
  - Doubt: Check meanings and tones.
- `p-oko-sound`: minimal pair (sound): o and ọ are different vowels.
  - Words: oko (farm), ọkọ (husband)
  - Doubt: Check meanings.
- `p-owo-sound`: minimal pair (sound): o and ọ are different vowels.
  - Words: owó (money), ọwọ́ (hand)
  - Doubt: Check meanings and tones.
- `p-ose-sound`: minimal pair (sound): s and ṣ are different sounds (tones differ too).
  - Words: ọ̀sẹ̀ (week), ọṣẹ (soap)
  - Doubt: Tones also differ, so not a pure sound pair; check.
- `tc-a`: tone comparison for **a**
  - Words (mid, high, low): wa (our), wá (come), wà (be, be located)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba section of the page "wa" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/<page>), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Check all three tones and meanings.
- `tc-e`: tone comparison for **e**
  - Words (mid, high, low): le (hard), lé (chase), lè (can)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba section of the page "le" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/<page>), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: The mid word le has several unrelated meanings on its page; check the mid tone and the chosen meaning.
- `tc-ee`: tone comparison for **ẹ**
  - Words (mid, high, low): bẹ (peel), bẹ́ (hop down), bẹ̀ (beg)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba section of the page "bẹ" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/<page>), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: The mid word bẹ has several unrelated meanings on its page; check the mid tone and the chosen meaning.
- `tc-i`: tone comparison for **i**
  - Words (mid, high, low): ṣi (make a mistake), ṣí (open), ṣì (become dull)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba section of the page "ṣi" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/<page>), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: The page writes ṣi (make a mistake) without a tone mark; check it is mid tone.
- `tc-o`: tone comparison for **o**
  - Words (mid, high, low): ro (hoe, till), ró (wrap a wrapper), rò (think)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba section of the page "ro" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/<page>), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: The mid word ro has several meanings on its page (hoe, ache); check the mid tone and the chosen meaning.
- `tc-oo`: tone comparison for **ọ**
  - Words (mid, high, low): kọ (write), kọ́ (build, teach), kọ̀ (refuse)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba section of the page "kọ" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/<page>), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Check all three tones and meanings. The kọ and kọ̀ clips are very short; listen to them in the app.
- `tc-u`: tone comparison for **u**
  - Words (mid, high, low): mu (drink), mú (take), mù (be hidden)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba section of the page "mu" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/<page>), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Check all three tones and meanings.
- `tc-an`: tone comparison for **an**
  - Words (mid, high, low): kan (one), kán (break), kàn (touch, concern)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba section of the page "kan" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/<page>), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Nasal vowel. The mid word kan is listed as a word for one; check the mid tone and meaning.
- `tc-in`: tone comparison for **in**
  - Words (mid, high, low): rin (grate), rín (smile, laugh), rìn (walk)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba section of the page "rin" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/<page>), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Nasal vowel. The mid word rin has several unrelated meanings on its page; check the mid tone and the chosen meaning.
- `tc-un`: tone comparison for **un**
  - Words (mid, high, low): sun (roast, cry), sún (move, shift), sùn (sleep)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba section of the page "sun" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/<page>), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Nasal vowel. The mid word sun has several unrelated meanings (roast, trickle, cry); check the mid tone and the chosen meaning.
- `tc-on`: tone comparison for **ọn**
  - Words (mid, high, low): wọn (their), wọ́n (expensive), wọ̀n (measure, weigh)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba section of the page "wọn" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/<page>), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Nasal vowel. wọ́n also means they (pronoun), and wọn is a possessive; check all three tones and the chosen meanings.
- `cs-gb-a-low`: consonant contrast (gb): Listen for gb: the g and b are said together with the lips closed. Compare it with plain b and g.
  - Words: bà (perch), gà (tickle), gbà (rescue, take)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba sections of the pages "ba", "ga", "gba" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/<page>), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Check the three words, their tones and the chosen meanings.
- `cs-gb-e-high`: consonant contrast (gb): Hear gé with a plain g, then gbé with g and b said together.
  - Words: gé (cut), gbé (carry)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba sections of the pages "ge", "gbe" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/<page>), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Check both words and the chosen meanings.
- `cs-p-o-high`: consonant contrast (p): Compare p, k, b and gb at the same vowel and tone. Yoruba p is said like kp, with the lips closed.
  - Words: pọ́ (inflate, pump), kọ́ (build), bọ́ (fall), gbọ́ (hear)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba sections of the pages "pọ", "kọ", "bọ", "gbọ" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/<page>), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Strict p minimal pairs are scarce, so this contrasts p with k, b and gb at one vowel and tone. Check the words, tones and chosen meanings.
- `cs-p-o-low`: consonant contrast (p): Compare p, k, b and g at the same vowel and tone. Yoruba p is said like kp, with the lips closed.
  - Words: pọ̀ (be plenty), kọ̀ (refuse), bọ̀ (come back), gọ̀ (be foolish)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba sections of the pages "pọ", "kọ", "bọ", "gọ" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/<page>), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Strict p minimal pairs are scarce, so this contrasts p with k, b and g at one vowel and tone. Check the words, tones and chosen meanings.
- `cs-s-sh-e-high`: consonant contrast (s-sh): Listen for s as in sun, then ṣ as in ship.
  - Words: sé (shut, block), ṣé (question word)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba sections of the pages "se", "ṣe" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/<page>), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: The gloss for ṣé comes from the app's own lesson (Ṣé o fẹ́ jẹun?), not the Wiktionary page. Check both words and meanings.
- `cs-s-sh-ee-high`: consonant contrast (s-sh): Listen for s as in sun, then ṣ as in ship.
  - Words: sẹ́ (sieve), ṣẹ́ (break)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba sections of the pages "sẹ", "ṣẹ" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/<page>), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Check both words and the chosen meanings.
- `cs-s-sh-ee-low`: consonant contrast (s-sh): Listen for s as in sun, then ṣ as in ship.
  - Words: sẹ̀ (condense), ṣẹ̀ (sin)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba sections of the pages "sẹ", "ṣẹ" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/<page>), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Check both words and the chosen meanings.

## Tone twins

- `i-town-oko`: **ọkọ̀** (vehicle)
  - Twins: ọkọ (husband), ọkọ́ (hoe), ọ̀kọ̀ (spear)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba section of the page "ọkọ" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/ọkọ), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Check all four tones and meanings.

  - Twins: ọkọ̀ (vehicle), ọkọ́ (hoe), ọ̀kọ̀ (spear)

## Proverbs

- `aaro-ki-i-gbona`: **Ààrò kì í gbóná kalẹ́.**
  - Word for word: The hearth does not stay hot until nighttime.
  - Meaning: Hard times do not last forever.
  - Wrong options: Cooking should be finished before dark. / A fire needs constant tending.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "aaro ki i gbona kalẹ" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary also lists the alternative form àdìrò kì í gbóná kalẹ́. Confirm the tones. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `aisi-nle-ekun`: **Àìsí-ńlé ẹkùn, ajá ń gbó.**
  - Word for word: In the leopard's absence from home, the dog barks.
  - Meaning: When the person in charge is away, others misbehave or grow bold.
  - Wrong options: Dogs are braver than leopards. / Leopards are afraid of dogs.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "aisi-nle ẹkun, aja n gbo" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary lists a variant, àìsí-ńlé ológbò, ilé dilé èkúté. Confirm the tones and the hyphen. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `aja-ki-i-roro`: **Ajá kì í rorò kó ṣọ́ ojúlé méjì.**
  - Word for word: A dog is never so fierce that it can guard two doorways.
  - Meaning: No one can serve two masters or give full attention to two demands at once.
  - Wrong options: Fierce dogs make the best guards. / Two houses are safer than one.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "aja ki i roro ko ṣọ ojule meji" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Confirm the tones, especially kó ṣọ́. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `ajo-ki-i-dun`: **Àjò kì í dùn kónílé má relé.**
  - Word for word: A journey cannot be so good that the homeowner does not return home.
  - Meaning: However well things go away from home, a person should not forget where they come from.
  - Wrong options: Travel is not worth the cost. / A house is only a place to sleep.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "ajo ki i dun konile ma rele" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary also gives àjò ò lè dùn kónílé má relé as an alternative form. Confirm the tones. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `agbada-o-ba-gbona`: **Bí agbada ò bá gbóná, àgbàdo ò lè ta.**
  - Word for word: If the pan is not hot, the corn will not pop.
  - Meaning: You cannot get a result without the effort or the cost it takes.
  - Wrong options: Cook corn in a hot pan only on market days. / A pan should be cleaned after every meal.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "bi agbada o ba gbona, agbado o le ta" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary lists two alternative forms (tí agbada ò bá gbóná... and bí agbada kò bá gbóná...). Confirm which wording speakers use. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `ewe-ba-pe-lara-ose`: **Bí ewé bá pẹ́ lára ọṣẹ, á dọṣẹ.**
  - Word for word: If a leaf stays on the soap long enough, it will become soap.
  - Meaning: People who spend a long time together begin to take on each other's ways.
  - Wrong options: Leaves wash clean when they touch soap. / Soap should be kept away from plants.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "bi ewe ba pẹ lara ọṣẹ, a dọṣẹ" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary's literal sense says the leaf is wrapped around the soap. Confirm the tones. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `majala-a-fo-wa-sile`: **Bí iná bá jó lóko, màjàlà á fò wá sílé.**
  - Word for word: When a fire burns in the field, the soot flies home.
  - Meaning: News of what happens far away always finds its way back home.
  - Wrong options: Fires on the farm should be put out quickly. / Soot is the sign of a good cook.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "bi ina ba jo loko, majala a fo wa sile" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary lists a second form ending ṣòfófó (the soot gossips). Confirm which form is standard and the tones. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `epe-la-fi-n-wo-epe`: **Èpè la fi ń wo èpè sàn.**
  - Word for word: Curses are what we use to cure curses.
  - Meaning: The remedy has to match the trouble. You answer like with like.
  - Wrong options: Never answer an insult. / Words cannot hurt anyone.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "epe la fi n wo epe san" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary glosses this as fighting fire with fire. Confirm the tones. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `ise-enia-nise-eranko`: **Ìṣe ènìyàn nìṣe ẹranko.**
  - Word for word: The actions of humans are the actions of animals.
  - Meaning: People behave much as animals do, so watching animals teaches you about people.
  - Wrong options: Humans and animals should never be kept together. / Animals are wiser than people.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "iṣe eniyan niṣe ẹranko" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary lists several alternative spellings (ìṣe èèyàn nìṣe ẹranko, ìṣe ènìyàn ni ìṣe ẹranko). Confirm the standard form. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `ka-rin-ka-po`: **Ká rìn ká pọ̀, yíyẹ ní ń yẹni.**
  - Word for word: Let us walk together; being fitting is what suits a person.
  - Meaning: People look their best when they live and work together, not alone.
  - Wrong options: Walking alone is faster. / Travelling in a group is slower.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "ka rin ka pọ, yiyẹ ni n yẹni" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary's literal sense reads: traveling in the company of others shows people in a good light. The word-for-word line here is the author's, so confirm it. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `omi-tuntun-ti-ru`: **Omi tuntun ti rú, ẹja tuntun ti wọ̀ ọ́.**
  - Word for word: New water has stirred, new fish have entered it.
  - Meaning: Every era brings its own fashions and its own people.
  - Wrong options: Fish cannot live in new water. / Rivers should be left undisturbed.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "omi tuntun ti ru, ẹja tuntun ti wọ ọ" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary lists the alternative forms omi tuntun ti dé, ẹja tuntun ti wọ̀ ọ́ and omi titun dé, ẹja titun wọ̀ ọ́. Confirm the tones. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `obe-to-dun`: **Ọbẹ̀ tó dùn, owó ló pa á.**
  - Word for word: A delicious soup, it is money that made it.
  - Meaning: Nothing good comes without money.
  - Wrong options: Soup tastes best when it is shared. / Money should never be spent on food.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "ọbẹ to dun, owo lo pa a" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary's literal sense reads: delicious soups are procured with money. The word-for-word line here is the author's, so confirm it. The verb pa (literally kill, strike) is read here in its idiom of making or procuring, so the word-for-word line says made. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `ona-kan-o-woja`: **Ọ̀nà kan ò wọjà.**
  - Word for word: One road alone does not enter the market.
  - Meaning: There is more than one way to reach a goal.
  - Wrong options: The market is best reached by the shortest road. / Roads to the market are always crowded.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "ọna kan o wọja" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary lists the alternative form ọ̀nà kan ò wọ ọjà. Confirm the tones, and note that the English line follows Wiktionary's literal sense: it is not one road only that leads to the market. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `sago-n-bugo`: **Ṣágo ń búgò.**
  - Word for word: The demijohn insults the bottle.
  - Meaning: Someone with the same fault is criticising another for it.
  - Wrong options: Whoever is bigger should be obeyed. / Bottles are worth more than demijohns.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "ṣago n bugo" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary glosses this as pot calling the kettle black. Confirm the tones. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `suuru-ni-baba-iwa`: **Sùúrù ni baba ìwà.**
  - Word for word: Patience is the father of character.
  - Meaning: Patience is the root of good character, and good conduct grows from it.
  - Wrong options: A patient man always becomes a father. / Character is something you are born with.
  - Source: Ling App, "Yoruba Proverbs" (ling-app.com/blog/yoruba-proverbs), read 2026-11-18, which lists it with this meaning.
  - Doubt: Ling App lists the wording and the meaning that good character grows from patience. Confirm the tones of ìwà and the word-for-word line.
- `iya-ni-wura`: **Ìyá ni wúrà, bàbá ni jígí.**
  - Word for word: A mother is gold, a father is a mirror.
  - Meaning: A mother is precious, and a father shows a child the person they may become.
  - Wrong options: Mothers are rich and fathers are vain. / Gold and mirrors are the best gifts for parents.
  - Source: Ling App, "Yoruba Proverbs" (ling-app.com/blog/yoruba-proverbs), read 2026-11-18, which lists it with this meaning.
  - Doubt: Ling App gives this wording with the sense that fathers reflect a child's future. Confirm whether jígí or dígí is standard, and the tones.
- `ogbon-ju-agbara-lo`: **Ọgbọ́n ju agbára lọ.**
  - Word for word: Wisdom is greater than strength.
  - Meaning: Careful thinking achieves more than force.
  - Wrong options: Strong people never need advice. / Wisdom comes only from heavy work.
  - Source: Ling App, "Yoruba Proverbs" (ling-app.com/blog/yoruba-proverbs), read 2026-11-18, which lists it with this meaning.
  - Doubt: Ling App writes Ọgbọ́n ju agbára without lọ. The lọ is added here because ju ... lọ is the comparing pattern (Intermediate 5). Confirm the wording and the tones.
- `ile-oba-to-jo`: **Ilé ọba tó jó, ẹwà ló bù sí.**
  - Word for word: The king's house that burned, beauty is what it gained.
  - Meaning: Hardship can lead to something better than what was lost.
  - Wrong options: Palaces should be built from stone, not wood. / Kings always rebuild at the cost of the poor.
  - Source: Ling App, "Yoruba Proverbs" (ling-app.com/blog/yoruba-proverbs), and Nigerian Finder, "Owe Yoruba: 100 Yoruba Proverbs & Their Meanings", both read 2026-11-18. Both give the sense of a burned palace rebuilt more beautiful.
  - Doubt: Ling App has tone marks but writes jọ (probably a slip for jó, burn); Nigerian Finder has none. Confirm the wording and the tones, especially bù sí.
- `agba-ki-i-wa-loja`: **Àgbà kì í wà lójà kí orí ọmọ tuntun wọ́.**
  - Word for word: An elder is never in the market while a newborn's head goes crooked.
  - Meaning: When wise elders are present, the young are not left to go wrong.
  - Wrong options: Elders spend too much time in the market. / A baby's head should not be touched in public.
  - Source: Nigerian Finder, "Owe Yoruba: 100 Yoruba Proverbs & Their Meanings", read 2026-11-18, which gives the untoned wording and a sense of correction by an elder's experience.
  - Doubt: The source has no tone marks, so every tone here is the author's. The word-for-word line and the meaning are the author's reading. Very high doubt: confirm.
- `diedie-imu-eledee`: **Díẹ̀díẹ̀ ni imú ẹlẹ́dẹ̀ fi ń wọgbà.**
  - Word for word: Little by little is how the pig's snout enters the farm.
  - Meaning: Something big, good or bad, begins as something small, so deal with small things early.
  - Wrong options: Pigs should be kept away from farms. / Pigs eat slowly because they are greedy.
  - Source: Nigerian Finder, "Owe Yoruba: 100 Yoruba Proverbs & Their Meanings", read 2026-11-18, which gives the sense of dealing with small problems before they grow.
  - Doubt: The source writes Díẹ̀ díẹ̀ nimú ẹlẹ́dẹ̀ẹ́ fi ń wọgbà. This form is the author's standard reading. Confirm the tones and whether ẹlẹ́dẹ̀ or ẹlẹ́dẹ̀ẹ́ is right.
- `a-n-pe-gbenagbena`: **À ń pe gbẹ́nàgbẹ́nà, àkókó ń yọjú.**
  - Word for word: We are calling the carpenter, the woodpecker is peeping in.
  - Meaning: Someone pushes into a matter they were not invited to join.
  - Wrong options: Carpenters work best with birds nearby. / Woodpeckers are better craftsmen than carpenters.
  - Source: Nigerian Finder, "Owe Yoruba: 100 Yoruba Proverbs & Their Meanings", read 2026-11-18, which gives the sense of overrating yourself and joining conversations you were not invited to.
  - Doubt: The source writes À ń pe gbẹ́nàgbẹ́nà ẹyẹ àkókó ń yọjú. The shortened form here is the author's. Confirm the wording and tones.
- `osan-ja-orun-dopa`: **Ọsán já, ọ̀run dọ̀pá.**
  - Word for word: The sun falls, the sky becomes a staff.
  - Meaning: On hearing of the death of a great person: how the mighty have fallen.
  - Wrong options: A good harvest will follow a bright afternoon. / A sign that the weather is about to change.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "ọsan ja ọrun dọpa" (Yoruba section), read 2026-11-18
  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives no literal line, only the parts ọsán, já, ọrun, di and ọ̀pá. The word-for-word line here is the author's assembly of those parts, so it is a high doubt. Confirm the tones, which the page marks only on the proverb line. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `di-endi-lopin-sinima`: **Dì ẹ́ǹdì lòpin sinimá.**
  - Word for word: "The end" is the end of the film show.
  - Meaning: Once something has ended, it is over for good.
  - Wrong options: Every story needs a sequel. / Films are better than real life.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "di ẹndi lopin sinima" (Yoruba section), read 2026-11-18
  - Doubt: Wiktionary glosses lòpin as ní òpin, and ẹ́ǹdì and sinimá are loanwords from English (end, cinema). Confirm the spelling and tones. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `owe-lesin-oro`: **Òwe lẹ́ṣin ọ̀rọ̀; bí ọ̀rọ̀ bá sọnù, òwe la fi ń wá a.**
  - Word for word: A proverb is the horse of speech; if speech is lost, it is with a proverb that we look for it.
  - Meaning: Proverbs carry meaning, and when words fail you, a proverb helps you find the right ones.
  - Wrong options: Horses carry messages between towns. / Only elders may use proverbs.
  - Source: The author's own knowledge of a widely quoted Yoruba saying; no consulted page gives it. Not checked with a speaker.
  - Doubt: A very widely quoted saying about proverbs themselves. Not found in the pages consulted for this batch. Confirm the wording and the tones of lẹ́ṣin and sọnù.
- `omode-ba-subu`: **Bí ọmọdé bá ṣubú, á wo iwájú; bí àgbà bá ṣubú, á wo ẹ̀yìn.**
  - Word for word: If a child falls, he looks ahead; if an elder falls, he looks behind.
  - Meaning: The young rush forward, while the old look back at the past and learn from it.
  - Wrong options: Elders fall more often than children. / Children get up faster than elders.
  - Source: The author's own knowledge of a widely quoted Yoruba saying; no consulted page gives it. Not checked with a speaker.
  - Doubt: A widely quoted saying. Not found in the pages consulted for this batch. The meaning is the author's reading. Confirm the wording and the tones.
- `omode-gbon-agba-gbon`: **Ọmọdé gbọ́n, àgbà gbọ́n, la fi dá Ilé-Ifẹ̀.**
  - Word for word: The child is wise, the elder is wise, this is how we founded Ile-Ife.
  - Meaning: A community thrives when the wisdom of the young and of the old are put together.
  - Wrong options: Ile-Ife was founded by children. / Young people should not question elders.
  - Source: The author's own knowledge of a widely quoted Yoruba saying; no consulted page gives it. Not checked with a speaker.
  - Doubt: A widely quoted saying about Ile-Ife. Not found in the pages consulted for this batch. Confirm the wording (la fi dá versus la fi ṣe) and the tones.
- `owo-omode-ko-to-pepe`: **Ọwọ́ ọmọdé kò tó pẹpẹ, ti àgbà kò wọ kèrègbè.**
  - Word for word: A child's hand does not reach the shelf; an elder's hand does not fit into the gourd.
  - Meaning: The young and the old each have limits, so they need each other.
  - Wrong options: Children should stay away from shelves. / Gourds are too small for any hand.
  - Source: The author's own knowledge of a widely quoted Yoruba saying; no consulted page gives it. Not checked with a speaker.
  - Doubt: A widely quoted saying. Not found in the pages consulted for this batch. Confirm the wording, the word kèrègbè (a narrow-necked gourd) and the tones.
- `ise-ni-oogun-ise`: **Iṣẹ́ ni oògùn ìṣẹ́.**
  - Word for word: Work is the medicine for poverty.
  - Meaning: Hard work is the cure for being poor.
  - Wrong options: Medicine costs money poor people lack. / Poverty is an illness doctors can treat.
  - Source: The author's own knowledge of a widely quoted Yoruba saying; no consulted page gives it. Not checked with a speaker.
  - Doubt: A widely quoted saying that plays on iṣẹ́ (work) and ìṣẹ́ (poverty). Not found in the pages consulted for this batch. Confirm the tones, which are the only difference between the two words.
- `iwa-lewa`: **Ìwà lẹ̀wà ọmọ ènìyàn.**
  - Word for word: Character is the beauty of a person.
  - Meaning: A person's real beauty is their good character, not their looks.
  - Wrong options: Beautiful people always have good manners. / Good character is hidden under a pretty face.
  - Source: The author's own knowledge of a widely quoted Yoruba saying; no consulted page gives it. Not checked with a speaker.
  - Doubt: A widely quoted saying (ìwà lẹ̀wà is a contraction of ìwà ni ẹ̀wà). Not found in the pages consulted for this batch. Confirm the wording and the tones.
- `omo-ti-a-ko-ko-proverb`: **Ọmọ tí a kò kọ́ ni yóò gbé ilé tí a kọ́ tà.**
  - Word for word: The child we did not teach is the one who will carry the house we built and sell it.
  - Meaning: A child who is not raised and taught well will waste what their parents worked to build.
  - Wrong options: Children should sell the house their parents built. / Teaching a child costs more than building a house.
  - Source: Afolayan, "Ọmọ Tí A Kò Kọ́: Globalization and Cultural Education among New Generation Nigerian Yorùbá", Yoruba Studies Review 3(1), 2021, quoted in lesson Mastery 4; translated "the child that is not taught will eventually sell the house that is built"
  - Doubt: Very high doubt: wording found in one source only, so confirm every tone. The social meaning is the author's reading of that translation. The proverb plays on kọ́ (teach) and kọ́ (build). The literal line is the author's.
- `oni-la-ri`: **Òní l'a rí, ọba òkè l'ó rọ́la.**
  - Word for word: Today is what we see; the King above (God) is the one who sees tomorrow.
  - Meaning: Only God knows what tomorrow brings, so no one can be sure of the future.
  - Wrong options: Wealth should be saved for tomorrow. / Rich people can buy a look at tomorrow.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "oni" (Yoruba section), quoting Awoyale, Global Yoruba Lexical Database v. 1.0 (Linguistic Data Consortium, 2008), translated "It is [only] today that we see, [only] the Most High sees tomorrow" and called a proverb on divine supremacy; quoted in lesson Mastery 4
  - Doubt: Very high doubt: one source. Confirm the wording, the apostrophe spelling (l' read as ni) and the tones. The social meaning follows the source's 'divine supremacy' label; the literal line and the used-when line are the author's.

## Transformations

- `neg-mo-lo` (negative): **Mo lọ** (I went)
  - Accepted answers: N kò lọ | N ò lọ | Mi ò lọ
  - Rule: kò goes before the verb. Before kò, mo becomes n (n kò, or n ò for short). Mi ò is the everyday form.
  - Source: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 1, Lesson 2, page 35 (kò goes before the verb) and Chapter 3, Lesson 2, page 76 (pronoun table: N kò, and kò alone for he or she). Wiktionary, "ko" and "o" (ò is a short form of kò after personal pronouns), "mi" and "ṣe" (Mi ò fẹ́ ṣe nǹkan kan, I don't want to do anything).
  - Doubt: The sentence is a new example. Model answer follows lesson b-2. Confirm n kò versus mi ò as the usual form, and the reading "did not go" versus "am not going".
- `q-o-fe-jeun` (question): **O fẹ́ jẹun** (You want to eat)
  - Accepted answers: Ṣé o fẹ́ jẹun?
  - Rule: Put ṣé in front of the statement. Nothing else moves.
  - Source: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 1, Lesson 4, page 41 (Ṣé o fẹ́ jẹun?) and the Lesson 1 dialogue, page 27 (Ṣé o sùn dáadáa?). Wiktionary, "ṣe" (ṣé before a statement makes a yes or no question) and "daadaa" (Ṣé ẹ sùn dáadáa?).
  - Doubt: The question is written in COERLL and the lessons. Tones are from Wiktionary and an encoding-damaged PDF, so confirm them.
- `fut-ade-jeun` (future): **Adé jẹun** (Adé ate)
  - Accepted answers: Adé máa jẹun | Adé yóò jẹun
  - Rule: Put máa (or yóò) before the verb to talk about what is going to happen.
  - Source: Wiktionary, Template "yo-aspects" (Ajani 2001): máa anticipative and yóò intentional; examples Ẹ̀yin yóò wá kí wa lọ́la, Èmi yóò máa lọ, A ti máa lọ kí o tó dé. Wiktionary, "rọ": Òjò máa rọ̀ (it will rain).
  - Doubt: The sentence is a new example. Wiktionary shows yóò mostly with pronouns, so confirm it with a name as subject.
- `past-mo-jeun` (past): **Mo ń jẹun** (I am eating)
  - Accepted answers: Mo jẹun
  - Rule: Drop ń. The bare verb means the action is done.
  - Source: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 1, Lesson 3, page 38 (without ń the verb means past: Mò ń lọ, Mo lọ). Wiktionary, "n" (Mo ń jẹun) and Template "yo-aspects" (unmarked verb is completed: mo lọ sí ilé-ìwé, I went to school; progressive Wọ́n ń ṣiṣẹ́).
  - Doubt: COERLL writes the pronoun with a low tone before ń (Mò ń jẹun), Wiktionary writes Mo. Confirm the reading "I ate".
- `neg-o-lo` (negative): **O lọ** (You went)
  - Accepted answers: O ò lọ | O kò lọ
  - Rule: kò goes before the verb. After o it is usually shortened to ò.
  - Source: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 1, Lesson 2, page 35 (kò goes before the verb) and Chapter 3, Lesson 2, page 76 (pronoun table: N kò, and kò alone for he or she). Wiktionary, "ko" and "o" (ò is a short form of kò after personal pronouns), "mi" and "ṣe" (Mi ò fẹ́ ṣe nǹkan kan, I don't want to do anything).
  - Doubt: The sentence is a new example. Confirm the tone on the shortened form after o.
- `q-o-sun` (question): **O sùn dáadáa** (You slept well)
  - Accepted answers: Ṣé o sùn dáadáa?
  - Rule: Put ṣé in front of the statement to ask yes or no.
  - Source: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 1, Lesson 4, page 41 (Ṣé o fẹ́ jẹun?) and the Lesson 1 dialogue, page 27 (Ṣé o sùn dáadáa?). Wiktionary, "ṣe" (ṣé before a statement makes a yes or no question) and "daadaa" (Ṣé ẹ sùn dáadáa?).
  - Doubt: The question is written in the COERLL dialogue. Confirm the tones on sùn and dáadáa.
- `fut-oja-ro` (future): **Òjò rọ̀** (It rained)
  - Accepted answers: Òjò máa rọ̀ | Òjò yóò rọ̀
  - Rule: Put máa (or yóò) before the verb for what will happen.
  - Source: Wiktionary, Template "yo-aspects" (Ajani 2001): máa anticipative and yóò intentional; examples Ẹ̀yin yóò wá kí wa lọ́la, Èmi yóò máa lọ, A ti máa lọ kí o tó dé. Wiktionary, "rọ": Òjò máa rọ̀ (it will rain).
  - Doubt: Òjò máa rọ̀ is written in Wiktionary. The yóò version is built from the aspect table and not found written out.
- `past-o-sere` (past): **Ó ń ṣeré** (He is playing)
  - Accepted answers: Ó ṣeré
  - Rule: Drop ń. The bare verb means the action is done.
  - Source: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 1, Lesson 3, page 38 (without ń the verb means past: Mò ń lọ, Mo lọ). Wiktionary, "n" (Mo ń jẹun) and Template "yo-aspects" (unmarked verb is completed: mo lọ sí ilé-ìwé, I went to school; progressive Wọ́n ń ṣiṣẹ́).
  - Doubt: Ó ń ṣeré is written in COERLL. Confirm the tones on ṣeré and the reading "he played".
- `neg-mo-fe-jeun` (negative): **Mo fẹ́ jẹun** (I want to eat)
  - Accepted answers: N kò fẹ́ jẹun | N ò fẹ́ jẹun | Mi ò fẹ́ jẹun
  - Rule: kò goes before the verb fẹ́. Before kò, mo becomes n (n kò, or n ò for short). Mi ò is the everyday form.
  - Source: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 1, Lesson 2, page 35 (kò goes before the verb) and Chapter 3, Lesson 2, page 76 (pronoun table: N kò, and kò alone for he or she). Wiktionary, "ko" and "o" (ò is a short form of kò after personal pronouns), "mi" and "ṣe" (Mi ò fẹ́ ṣe nǹkan kan, I don't want to do anything).
  - Doubt: Built from Mi ò fẹ́ ṣe nǹkan kan (Wiktionary) and N kò fẹ́ jẹ búrẹ́dì (COERLL). Not found written out as given.
- `q-e-sun` (question): **Ẹ sùn dáadáa** (You (plural or respectful) slept well)
  - Accepted answers: Ṣé ẹ sùn dáadáa?
  - Rule: Put ṣé in front of the statement to ask yes or no.
  - Source: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 1, Lesson 4, page 41 (Ṣé o fẹ́ jẹun?) and the Lesson 1 dialogue, page 27 (Ṣé o sùn dáadáa?). Wiktionary, "ṣe" (ṣé before a statement makes a yes or no question) and "daadaa" (Ṣé ẹ sùn dáadáa?).
  - Doubt: Wiktionary writes Ṣé ẹ sùn dáadáa? as an example. Confirm the tones.
- `fut-mo-ilewe` (future): **Mo lọ sí ilé-ìwé** (I went to school)
  - Accepted answers: Mo máa lọ sí ilé-ìwé | Èmi máa lọ sí ilé-ìwé | Mà á lọ sí ilé-ìwé | Èmi yóò lọ sí ilé-ìwé
  - Rule: máa goes before the verb, with a short or long pronoun. With yóò, only the long pronoun èmi is used.
  - Source: Wiktionary, Template "yo-aspects" (Ajani 2001): máa anticipative and yóò intentional; examples Ẹ̀yin yóò wá kí wa lọ́la, Èmi yóò máa lọ, A ti máa lọ kí o tó dé. Wiktionary, "rọ": Òjò máa rọ̀ (it will rain).
  - Doubt: The past sentence is written in Wiktionary. The future forms are built from the aspect table. Mà á lọ is listed by COERLL (lesson i-1). Confirm Mo máa versus Èmi yóò.
- `past-ade-mu-omi` (past): **Adé ń mu omi** (Adé is drinking water)
  - Accepted answers: Adé mu omi
  - Rule: Drop ń. The bare verb means the action is done.
  - Source: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 1, Lesson 3, page 38 (without ń the verb means past: Mò ń lọ, Mo lọ). Wiktionary, "n" (Mo ń jẹun) and Template "yo-aspects" (unmarked verb is completed: mo lọ sí ilé-ìwé, I went to school; progressive Wọ́n ń ṣiṣẹ́).
  - Doubt: Adé ń mu omi is written in COERLL. Confirm the reading "Adé drank water".
- `neg-ade-sun` (negative): **Adé sùn** (Adé slept)
  - Accepted answers: Adé kò sùn
  - Rule: kò goes before the verb. After a name it stays kò.
  - Source: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 1, Lesson 2, page 35 (kò goes before the verb) and Chapter 3, Lesson 2, page 76 (pronoun table: N kò, and kò alone for he or she). Wiktionary, "ko" and "o" (ò is a short form of kò after personal pronouns), "mi" and "ṣe" (Mi ò fẹ́ ṣe nǹkan kan, I don't want to do anything).
  - Doubt: The sentence is a new example. Confirm that a name before kò is not shortened.
- `q-ade-mu-omi` (question): **Adé ń mu omi** (Adé is drinking water)
  - Accepted answers: Ṣé Adé ń mu omi?
  - Rule: Put ṣé in front of the statement. The ń stays.
  - Source: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 1, Lesson 4, page 41 (Ṣé o fẹ́ jẹun?) and the Lesson 1 dialogue, page 27 (Ṣé o sùn dáadáa?). Wiktionary, "ṣe" (ṣé before a statement makes a yes or no question) and "daadaa" (Ṣé ẹ sùn dáadáa?).
  - Doubt: The statement is written in COERLL. The question is built from the ṣé pattern and not found written out.
- `fut-eyin-wa` (future): **Ẹ̀yin wá kí wa** (You came to greet us)
  - Accepted answers: Ẹ̀yin máa wá kí wa | Ẹ̀yin yóò wá kí wa | Ẹ máa wá kí wa
  - Rule: máa or yóò goes before the verb. yóò needs the long pronoun ẹ̀yin; máa takes ẹ̀yin or ẹ.
  - Source: Wiktionary, Template "yo-aspects" (Ajani 2001): máa anticipative and yóò intentional; examples Ẹ̀yin yóò wá kí wa lọ́la, Èmi yóò máa lọ, A ti máa lọ kí o tó dé. Wiktionary, "rọ": Òjò máa rọ̀ (it will rain).
  - Doubt: Wiktionary writes Ẹ̀yin yóò wá kí wa lọ́la. The máa version with the short pronoun ẹ is built from the pattern.
- `past-won-sise` (past): **Wọ́n ń ṣiṣẹ́** (They are working)
  - Accepted answers: Wọ́n ṣiṣẹ́
  - Rule: Drop ń. The bare verb means the action is done.
  - Source: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 1, Lesson 3, page 38 (without ń the verb means past: Mò ń lọ, Mo lọ). Wiktionary, "n" (Mo ń jẹun) and Template "yo-aspects" (unmarked verb is completed: mo lọ sí ilé-ìwé, I went to school; progressive Wọ́n ń ṣiṣẹ́).
  - Doubt: Wọ́n ń ṣiṣẹ́ is written in Wiktionary. Confirm the reading "they worked".
- `neg-eyin-wa` (negative): **Ẹ wá** (You (plural) came)
  - Accepted answers: Ẹ ò wá | Ẹ kò wá
  - Rule: kò goes before the verb. After ẹ it is usually shortened to ò.

Wiktionary, "ko" and "o" (ò is a short form of kò after personal pronouns), "mi" and "ṣe" (Mi ò fẹ́ ṣe nǹkan kan, I don't want to do anything).
  - Doubt: The sentence is a new example. Confirm the shortened form and the tone on wá.
- `q-o-lo-ile` (question): **O lọ sí ilé** (You went home)
  - Accepted answers: Ṣé o lọ sí ilé?
  - Rule: Put ṣé in front of the statement to ask yes or no.

  - Doubt: Built from the ṣé pattern and the lọ sí ilé phrase in the lessons. Not found written out.
- `fut-a-lo` (future): **A lọ** (We went)
  - Accepted answers: A máa lọ | Àwa máa lọ | Àwa yóò lọ
  - Rule: máa goes before the verb, with a short or long pronoun. With yóò, only the long pronoun àwa is used.
  - Source: Wiktionary, Template "yo-aspects" (Ajani 2001): máa anticipative and yóò intentional; examples Ẹ̀yin yóò wá kí wa lọ́la, Èmi yóò máa lọ, A ti máa lọ kí o tó dé. Wiktionary, "rọ": Òjò máa rọ̀ (it will rain).
  - Doubt: Wiktionary writes A ti máa lọ kí o tó dé and Àwa yóò ti lọ. The exact forms are built from the table.
- `past-mo-lo` (past): **Mo ń lọ** (I am going)
  - Accepted answers: Mo lọ
  - Rule: Drop ń. The bare verb means the action is done.
  - Source: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 1, Lesson 3, page 38 (without ń the verb means past: Mò ń lọ, Mo lọ). Wiktionary, "n" (Mo ń jẹun) and Template "yo-aspects" (unmarked verb is completed: mo lọ sí ilé-ìwé, I went to school; progressive Wọ́n ń ṣiṣẹ́).
